Solutions with one dimensional concentration for a two dimensional Gross-Pitaevskii model with general potential Thanks: § Corresponding author: Jun Yang, jyang2019@gzhu.edu.cn
Lipeng Duan, Suting Wei, Jun Yang §

Abstract
. We concern standing wave solutions with frequency λ \lambda to a two dimensional Gross-Pitaevskii equation with a trap potential under the unit mass constraint, which is used to describe Bose-Einstein condensates with attractive interaction. First, we investigate the necessary conditions for existence of the solutions with concentration phenomena directed along closed smooth curves. Next, not only imposing stationary and non-degeneracy conditions on the curves with respect to an auxiliary weighted length involving the trap potential, but also adding some other technical assumptions, we select a sequence { λ j } \{\lambda_{j}\} of the frequency λ \lambda with − λ j → + ∞ -\lambda_{j}\rightarrow+\infty and construct solutions with concentration directed along the curves. Our result partially answers the conjecture raised in [A. Ambrosetti, A. Malchiodi, W.-M. Ni, Comm. Math. Phys. 2003] about necessary condition for solution concentrating at submanifolds. The solutions constructed in this paper are concentrating on curves whose length are non-uniformly bounded, and hence the situation is quite different from that in [M. del Pino, M. Kowalczyk, J. Wei, Comm. Pure Appl. Math. 2007]. Keywords: Gross-Pitaevskii model, L 2 L^{2} -mass constraint, General potential, Concentration on curves AMS Subject Classification: 35A01, 35B09, 35B25.



1.
Introduction

1.1.
The problem with a constraint

Consider the time-dependent Gross-Pitaevskii equation

\displaystyle i\frac{\partial\Psi}{\partial{\mathfrak{t}}}=-\Delta\Psi+W({\bf y})\Psi-a|\Psi|^{2}\Psi,\quad\forall\,({\mathfrak{t}},{\bf y})\in{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}^{N},\quad N\geq 2

(1.1)

with a mass constraint

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}|\Psi|^{2}=1,

(1.2)

where
i
is the imaginary unit,
a>0
represents the attractive interaction strength and
W({\bf y})
is the external trap potential.
This kind of equation (
1.1
) with the mass constraint (
1.2
) is a
well-known mathematical model to describe Bose-Einstein condensates

[
1
,
2
,
15
,
17
,
27
,
37
,
39
,
45
,
46
]
.

If we look for a standing wave solution of (
1.1
)-(
1.2
) with the form

\Psi({\mathfrak{t}},{\bf y})=\tilde{u}({\bf y})e^{-i\lambda{\mathfrak{t}}}

where
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
is a frequency parameter to be determined and
\tilde{u}
is an unknown function,
then the pair
(\lambda,\tilde{u})
must satisfy the following time-independent equation

\displaystyle-\Delta\tilde{u}+W({\bf y})\tilde{u}-a|\tilde{u}|^{2}\tilde{u}=\lambda\tilde{u},\quad\forall\,{\bf y}\in\mathbb{R}^{N},

(1.3)

with the mass constraint

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}|\tilde{u}|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}{\bf y}=1.

(1.4)

We also know that a ground state solution of (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) is a minimizer of the following problem

\displaystyle E_{a}:=\min_{u\in\mathscr{M}}J_{a}(u),

(1.5)

where

J_{a}(u)=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}\Big[\,\frac{1}{2}|\nabla u|^{2}+\frac{1}{2}W({\bf y})u^{2}-\frac{a}{4}|u|^{4}\,\Big]{\mathrm{d}}{\bf y},

and

\mathscr{M}=\Big\{u\in H^{1}({\mathbb{R}}^{N})\,:\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}|u|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}{\bf y}=1\,\text{and}\,\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}W({\bf y})|u|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}{\bf y}<+\infty\Big\}.

Readers can refer to
[
8
,
9
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
]
on the ground state solutions of Gross-Pitaevskii equation.
On the other hand, any solution of (
1.3
) whose energy is greater than the ground state is called excited state in the physical literature
[
7
]
.

Recently, P. Luo, S. Peng, J. Wei and S. Yan
[
41
]
studied problem (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) in
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}

(N=2,3)
,
and showed that, if
\tilde{u}_{a}
is a solution to problem (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) concentrating at some points as
a\rightarrow{\bf a}
, then there must have been

{\bf a}\geq 0
and
\lambda\to-\infty,
as
a\to{\bf a}
,

where
{\bf a}=ka_{*}
for some integer
k
if
N=2
, or
{\bf a}=0
if
N=3
. Here

a_{*}=\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}|Q|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}{\bf y},

and
Q
is the unique positive solution to the equation

-\Delta Q+Q-Q^{3}=0

in
\mathbb{R}^{2}.

In the present paper,
we will consider the asymptotic behavior of excited state solutions
\tilde{u}
to problem (
1.3
)-(
1.4
), as
a\to{\bf a},
with some
{\bf a}
to be determined later, which exhibit a higher dimensional concentration phenomenon. According to the analysis in
[
29
]
and
[
41
]
, throughout this paper, we make the assumption that

\displaystyle-\lambda\rightarrow+\infty,\quad\text{as $a\to{\bf a}$}.

(1.6)

Letting

\displaystyle\tilde{u}=\sqrt{\frac{-\lambda}{a}}u,

then we get that
u
satisfies

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{\lambda}\Delta u-\Big[1-\frac{1}{\lambda}W({\bf y})\Big]u+|u|^{2}u=0,\quad\forall\,{\bf y}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}.

Taking

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}=\frac{1}{-\lambda},

(1.7)

we will transform (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) to

\displaystyle-\epsilon^{2}\Delta u+\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W({\bf y})\big]u-|u|^{2}u=0,\quad\forall\,{\bf y}\in\mathbb{R}^{N},

(1.8)

and

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{N}}|u|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}{\bf y}=a\epsilon^{2}.

(1.9)

The assumption (
1.6
) is equivalent to the following fact

\displaystyle\epsilon\to 0,\quad\text{as\,$a\to{\bf a}$}.

(1.10)

The studies on problem (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) (or (
1.8
)-(
1.9
)), such as concentration, uniqueness and so on, have been the object of investigation of several researchers. We refer the readers to the papers
[
28
,
29
,
31
,
32
,
41
,
44
]
and the references therein.

1.2.
The singular perturbation problem

Here we mention that elliptic problem (
1.8
) is closely related to the following singular perturbation problem

\displaystyle-\epsilon^{2}\Delta u+V({\bf y})u-|u|^{p-1}u=0,\quad u>0,\quad u\in H^{1}(\Omega),\quad\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{N},

(1.11)

where
1<p<2^{*}-1,

\epsilon>0
is a small parameter, and
V
(independent of
\epsilon
) is the potential.
Problem (
1.11
) appears in many applications, for instance in chemotaxis
[
40
]
and biological pattern formation
[
26
]
.
Problem (
1.11
) with Dirichlet or Neumann boundary conditions were studied
extensively in recent years, involving the necessary and sufficient for the concentration.
See
[
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
14
,
16
,
18
,
19
,
21
,
24
,
30
,
47
,
49
,
50
,
51
]

for backgrounds and references about problem (
1.11
).
More generally, for the complex value cases, the concentration of solution for the singular perturbation problem

\displaystyle-\epsilon^{2}\Delta u+V({\bf y})u-|u|^{p-1}u=0,\quad u\in\mathbb{C},\quad u\in H^{1}(\Omega;\mathbb{C}),\quad\Omega\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{N},

(1.12)

was also studied.
In
[
42
]
, F. Mahmoudi, A. Malchiodi and M. Montenegro exhibited the complex value solutions which concentrate along curves and whose phases are highly oscillatory. L. Wang and C. Zhao in
[
48
]
studied the nonlinear Schrödinger problem with electromagnetic potential and showed the existence of solution which concentrates at a closed curve.

2.
Main results

Inspired by the previous discussions, the main objective of the present paper is to study problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with the assumption (
1.6
)
\,(i.e.\,\eqref{epsilonto0}\,)
from two perspectives:

\clubsuit

We want to investigate some necessary conditions for the existence of a solution with concentration phenomenon directing along a closed curve.

\clubsuit

For problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) with non-symmetric potential, we will impose some reasonable conditions on the potential and then construct a solution concentrating at a curve by choosing
\lambda

(
i.e. the parameter
\epsilon
)
.

2.1.
Necessary conditions for solutions with concentration

In this part, we present some results about the necessary conditions of the concentration
phenomenon happening for problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) and the singular perturbation problem (
1.11
).

2.1.1.
Necessary conditions for concentration phenomena to the singular perturbation problem

For problem (
1.11
), if the concentrations take place at some points
x_{\epsilon,j}
, then
\nabla V(x_{\epsilon,j})=0,
see
[
5
,
6
,
14
,
18
,
19
,
24
]
. On the other hand, the necessary conditions for the higher dimensional concentration were also studied.
We now recall the following proposition concerning the radially symmetric case.

Proposition A
. (
[
3
]
)

Let
\Omega={\mathbb{R}}^{N}
in (
1.11
), and the function
V
is radial.
Suppose that problem (
1.11
) has a radial solution which concentrates at the sphere
|{\bf y}|=\bar{r}
,
then
\bar{r}
is a critical point of
M(r)=r^{N-1}V^{\sigma}(r)
with
\sigma=\frac{p+1}{p-1}-\frac{1}{2}
.
∎

A rather complicated but interesting problem is to determine the necessary conditions for the non-radial concentration phenomena of higher dimension for problem (
1.11
).
The necessary condition for the existence of a non-radial solution with concentration on
k
-dimensional submanifold
\Sigma
was derived formally in Section 8 (Open Problems and Perspectives) in
[
3
]
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\bigg[\frac{p+1}{p-1}-\frac{N-k}{2}\bigg]\nabla^{\perp}V=V\mathbf{H}\quad\mbox{on }\Sigma,

(2.1)

where
\mathbf{H}
is the mean curvature of
\Sigma

and
\nabla^{\perp}
denotes the component of
V
normal to
\Sigma
.
This was exemplified in
[
21
]
,
[
51
]
and also
[
47
]
.
However, to the best of our knowledge, there is no rigorous proof to (
2.1
) in the available literature.
This will be addressed in Corollary
2.3
for the two dimensional case.

2.1.2.
Necessary conditions for solutions with concentration to problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
)

Let’s go back to problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
).
In the recent work
[
29
]
, Q. Guo, S. Tian and Y. Zhou considered problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with
W({\bf y})=W(|{\bf y}|)

and proved the necessary conditions for radial solutions concentrating at spheres.
More precisely,

if a radial solution
u_{\epsilon}
of the problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
)
concentrated at a sphere
r=r_{\epsilon}
with
r=|{\bf y}|
, then there must have been

r_{\epsilon}\to+\infty,\quad\text{and}\quad M_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(r_{\epsilon})\to 0,\quad\text{as}\,\epsilon\to 0,

where
M_{\epsilon}(r)=r\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(r)\big]^{\frac{3}{2}}
.

We note, in
[
29
]
the symmetry reduces the technical difficulties and allows the authors to handle the problem by ODE methods.

A strong motivation of the current work is to give some necessary conditions for solutions with concentration to (
1.8
)-(
1.9
), without imposing any symmetry assumption on the trapping potential
W
.
To avoid complicated calculations, we here only consider the two dimensional case, i.e.,
N=2
,
with concentration directed along a smooth simple closed curve
\Gamma_{\epsilon}\subseteq{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
More precisely, the following problems under the assumption (
1.6
) will be addressed:

\bullet
If
u_{\epsilon}
is a non-radial solution to (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) with higher dimensional concentration
(
see the form in (
2.11
)-(
2.14
)
)
as
a\to{\bf a}
, then what’s the value of
{\bf a}
?
What’s the limit of
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
when
a\to{\bf a}

(
or
\epsilon\to 0

)
?
What necessary conditions shall the potential
W
and
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
satisfy?

Note that in the present work, the curves
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
depend on the parameter
\epsilon
.
For the above purpose, the following are the settings to avoid some tedious computations and make mathematical analysis rigorous.
Here are the assumptions in the first one for the curves, which are the concentration sets of the solutions.

(A1).

Assume that
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
are simple smooth closed curves in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Consider the natural parameterization
\gamma_{\epsilon}(\theta)
of
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
with positive orientation, where
\theta
denotes the arclength parameter measured from a fixed point of
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
.
We denote

\displaystyle\ell=|\Gamma_{\epsilon}|,

(2.2)

and the curvature of
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
by
\kappa(\theta)
.
We also assume that there exists a positive constant
C_{0}
independent of
\epsilon
such that

\displaystyle|\kappa(\theta)|,\,|\kappa^{\prime}(\theta)|\leq C_{0},\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,\ell).

(2.3)

The assumption
-C_{0}\leq\kappa(\theta)\leq C_{0}
in (
2.3
) implies that
there exists a small positive number
M_{0}
independent of
\epsilon
(which can be chosen such that
C_{0}M_{0}<1
) in such a way that any point
{\bf y}
near
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
can be represented in
Fermi coordinates

\displaystyle{\bf y}=\gamma_{\epsilon}(\theta)+t\nu_{\epsilon}(\theta),\quad\forall\,t\in(-M_{0},M_{0}),\ \forall\,\theta\in[0,\ell),

(2.4)

where
\nu_{\epsilon}(\theta)
denotes the unit outer normal to
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
.
From (
2.4
), we know that the map

{\bf y}\mapsto(t,\theta)
is a local diffeomorphism (see
[
25
]
, Sec. 14.6). By a slight abuse use of notation, for
{\bf y}
in (
2.4
) and any function
W
, we denote
W(t,\theta)
to actually mean
W({\bf y})
.
We now can propose the assumptions for the setting of the potential function
W
.

(A2).

The smooth potential function
W
is positive on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
Moreover, there exist two positive constants
C
and
\varrho<1
independent of
\epsilon
, such that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\ell}\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta\,\leq\,C\epsilon^{\varrho-1},

(2.5)

\displaystyle\max\big\{\,\epsilon^{2}|W_{t}(t,\theta)|,\ \epsilon^{2}|W_{tt}(t,\theta)|\,\big\}\leq C\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big],\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,\ell),\ \forall\,t\in(-M_{0},M_{0}),

(2.6)

and

\displaystyle\max\Big\{\,\epsilon^{2}|W_{\theta}(0,\theta)|,\ \epsilon^{2}|W_{\theta\theta}(0,\theta)|,\ \,\epsilon^{2}|W_{\theta\theta\theta}(0,\theta)|,\ \,\epsilon^{2}|W_{t\theta}(0,\theta)|,

\displaystyle\quad\quad\quad\,\epsilon^{2}|W_{tt\theta}(0,\theta)|,\ \,\epsilon^{2}|W_{t\theta\theta}(0,\theta)|\,\Big\}\,\leq\,C\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big],\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,\ell).

(2.7)

Remark 2.1
.

Note that we need the assumptions in (
2.6
) and (
2.1.2
) such that some estimates can be carried out.
The readers can refer to (
3.14
)-(
3.17
), the proof of Lemma
4.3
and also the last paragraph in Section
3.2
.
On the other hand, we will use (
2.5
) to deal with the complicated resonance involved in the higher dimensional non-radial concentration for problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
), which will be clarified in Remark
2.7
.
∎

Our first goal is to find necessary conditions for the existence of a concentrated solution
u_{\epsilon}
to problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
),
whose properties can be described in the following way.

We denote by
U
the unique positive solution to the problem

\displaystyle U^{\prime\prime}-U+U^{3}=0\quad\text{for }\,x\in{\mathbb{R}},\qquad U^{\prime}(0)=0,\qquad U(\pm\infty)=0,

(2.8)

and also define a positive function
\beta>1
in the form

\displaystyle\beta(\theta)\,=\,\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}},

(2.9)

as well as a smooth cut-off function
\chi
by

\displaystyle\chi(t)=1\quad\mbox{if}\ |t|<M_{1},\qquad\chi(t)=0\quad\mbox{if}\ |t|>2M_{1}:=M_{2},

(2.10)

where
M_{2}<M_{0}
with
M_{0}
given in (
2.4
).
Suppose that

\displaystyle u_{\epsilon}({\bf y})={\mathbf{u}}({\bf y})+\phi({\bf y})

(2.11)

is a solution with concentration on
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
to problem (
1.8
).
In other words, the function
{\mathbf{u}}
has the following local form in the Fermi coordinates
(t,\theta)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}(t,\theta)\,=\,\chi(t)\beta(\theta)\,U\big(\beta(\theta)t/\epsilon\big).

(2.12)

Moreover, the requirements for
\phi
can be listed in the forms

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{\partial^{J}\phi}{\partial t^{J_{1}}\partial\theta^{J_{2}}}\Big\|_{L^{2}\big(\{|t|<M_{2}\}\big)}=\frac{O\big(\epsilon^{\varrho}\big)}{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\ell}|\beta(\theta)|^{5}\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta}\Big\|\frac{\partial^{J}{\mathbf{u}}}{\partial t^{J_{1}}\partial\theta^{J_{2}}}\Big\|_{L^{2}\big(\{|t|<M_{2}\}\big)},

(2.13)

\displaystyle\Big\|\frac{\partial^{j}\phi}{\partial\theta^{j}}\Big\|_{L^{2}\big(\{|t|<M_{2}\}\big)}=\frac{O\big(\epsilon^{\varrho}\big)}{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\ell}|\beta(\theta)|^{5}\,{\mathrm{d}}\theta}\Big\|\frac{\partial^{j}{\mathbf{u}}}{\partial\theta^{j}}\Big\|_{L^{2}\big(\{|t|<M_{2}\}\big)},

(2.14)

where
\varrho<1
is the positive constant in (
2.5
),
j
is an index with
2\leq j\leq 3
and
J=(J_{1},J_{2})
is an index of two components with
|J|\leq 1
or
J=(1,1)
.

Now we give our first result of the present paper.

Theorem 2.2
.

Suppose that: problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) has a solution
u_{\epsilon}
which concentrates at the smooth simple closed curve
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
,
i.e. the solution
u_{\epsilon}
enjoys the decomposition (
2.11
) plus the requirements in (
2.12
)-(
2.14
).
If (
1.6
) and the assumptions in
(A1)
-
(A2)
hold the validity, then we have the following results

(1).

There exists a positive constant
\ell_{0}
independent of
\epsilon
such that

\displaystyle\ell\,\geq\,\ell_{0},\qquad\forall\,\epsilon>0.

(2.15)

(2).

For the
\bf a
given in (
1.6
), there holds

\displaystyle{\bf a}=+\infty.

(2.16)

(3).

The following relation holds:

\displaystyle\frac{3\epsilon^{2}W_{t}(0,\theta)}{\,2\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]\,}\,+\,\kappa(\theta)\,=\,o(1)\qquad\mbox{as}\ \epsilon\rightarrow 0,

(2.17)

for all
\theta\in[0,\ell)
.
∎

Note that, by using a similar method as the proof of Theorem
2.2
,
we can prove the necessary condition in (
2.1
) for the solution concentrating at a closed smooth curve
\Gamma\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
to singular perturbation problem (
1.11
) in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
.
This case is much more easier than Theorem
2.2
, since the limit set
\Gamma
is independent of
\epsilon
.
The result will be provided in the following corollary, which is clearly an extension of
Proposition A
given by A. Ambrosetti, A. Malchiodi and W.-M. Ni in
[
3
]
.

Corollary 2.3
.

Suppose that problem (
1.11
) in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
has a solution which concentrates at a closed smooth curve
\Gamma
,
then the identity holds

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{p+1}{p-1}-\frac{1}{2}\Big)V_{t}(0,\theta)=-\kappa(\theta)V(0,\theta),\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,\ell),

where
\kappa(\theta)
is the curvature of
\Gamma
, and
(t,\theta)
are the adapted version of Fermi coordinates for
{\bf y}
near
\Gamma
.
∎

2.2.
Existence of solutions with concentration phenomena

We now consider the converse of Theorem
2.2
: consider problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) with (
1.10
) and construct solutions concentrating at a curve
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
which satisfies the necessary conditions in (
2.17
).

Since the pioneering work by A. Floer and A. Weinstein
[
24
]
, to construct concentrated solutions for problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) or singular perturbation problem (
1.11
) has been a topic of general interest in the study of singular perturbation theory,
including cases concentrating on points or
k
-dimensional submanifolds, with
1\leq k\leq N-1
.

For the construction of a solution to problem (
1.11
) concentrating at higher dimensional submanifold of
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
,
we mention that in
[
3
]
, A. Ambrosetti, A. Malchiodi, and W.-M. Ni considered (
1.11
) in
{\mathbb{R}}^{N}
with radial potential
V
and showed the existence of higher-dimensional layer solutions.
Furthermore, M. del Pino, M. Kowalczyk, and J. Wei
[
21
]
studied (
1.11
) in
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
without any symmetry hypothesis on the potential
V
.
By using infinite Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction, a non-radial solution
u_{\epsilon}
was constructed, which concentrates at a closed stationary and non-degenerate curve for the weighted functional
\int_{\Gamma}V^{\sigma}
with
\sigma=\frac{p+1}{p-1}-\frac{1}{2}
,
provided that
\epsilon
is small and satisfies a gap condition due to the resonance character of the problem.
See also
[
47
]
for solutions concentrating on hypersurfaces.

Here we will mention some works to the constructions of solutions to problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
).
In
[
41
]
, P. Luo, S. Peng, J. Wei and S. Yan considered (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) with a class of degenerate trapping potential with non-isolated critical points,
and showed that the existence and local uniqueness of excited states by accurately analyzing the location of the concentrated points and the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
.
Recently, in
[
29
]
, Q. Guo, S. Tian and Y. Zhou considered the case that
W
is radially symmetric and studied concentration phenomena of solutions to problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
).
By using a finite dimensional Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction method and blow-up analysis based on Pohozaev identity, they showed the existence of radial solutions concentrating on a circle with radius
r_{\epsilon}\to+\infty
as
\epsilon\to 0
.
Here we point out that the symmetry condition of
W
in
[
29
]
reduces the problem to just adjusting one parameter
r_{\epsilon}
representing the location of the concentration curve and a finite-dimensional Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction was used.

It’s natural to ask whether we could remove the symmetry condition of
W
imposed in
[
29
]
.
When
W
is non-radial, we are unable to transform the problem into a problem of ODE, and whence the current problem is fundamentally different from that in
[
29
]
involving radial potential.
On the other hand, we know that, for the semi-linear problem with non-symmetric potential, the gluing methods provided in
[
21
]
make it possible to construct a solution concentrating at a curve. In general, for semi-linear elliptic problem like (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) or (
1.11
), the situation of adjusting a finite number of points
(
finite dimensional Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction
), and that of adjusting a higher dimensional object such as a geodesic in suitable metrics as limiting concentrating sets (
infinite Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction
) will be treated.
However, in contrast to the situations of bounded concentration sets in
[
21
]
(as well as the corresponding references
[
42
,
47
,
48
,
49
,
50
,
51
]
), the concentration directed along curves
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
with non-uniformly bounded length will lead to some atypical challenges, see Remark
2.7
.

In the present paper, inspired by
[
21
]
, we will consider problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) with a non-symmetric potential
W
and construct a solution that concentrates at a curve
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, to make an extension of the results in
[
29
]
.
In addition to the assumptions in
(A1)
-
(A2)
, by recalling the Fermi coordinates in (
2.4
),
the following assumptions in
(B1)
-
(B3)
will be imposed.
We note that these conditions will play a crucial role in the reduction process of constructing solutions concentrated on the curve
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
.

The following definitions are adapted from
[
21
]
.
Recall the Fermi coordinates defined as in (
2.4
). For
\epsilon
small, any curve
\Gamma^{g}_{\epsilon}
close to
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
can be parameterized as

\displaystyle\gamma_{\epsilon}^{g}(\theta)=\gamma_{\epsilon}(\theta)+g(\theta)\nu_{\epsilon}(\theta),\quad\theta\in[0,\ell).

Following the analysis and computations as in
[
21
]
, we can easily get

\displaystyle J_{\epsilon}(g)

\displaystyle=\int_{\Gamma_{\epsilon}^{g}}\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(g,\theta)\big]^{\frac{3}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\sigma

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\ell}\Big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(g,\theta)\Big]^{3/2}\,\Big[(1+\kappa g)^{2}+|g^{\prime}|^{2}\Big]^{1/2}{\mathrm{d}}\theta.

In the present paper, for any fixed small
\epsilon
, we just make the
stationary assumption
for the curve
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
, which means that the first variation of the functional
J_{\epsilon}(g)
at
g=0
equals to
0
.
In other words, for any smooth,
\ell
-periodic function
h(\theta)
, there must be

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\ell}\Bigg\{\frac{3}{2}\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{1/2}\epsilon^{2}W_{t}(0,\theta)\ +\ \big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{3}{2}}\kappa\Bigg\}h{\mathrm{d}}\theta=0,

which is equivalent to the following condition

\displaystyle\kappa(\theta)\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]+\frac{3}{2}\epsilon^{2}W_{t}(0,\theta)=0,\quad\forall\,\theta\in[0,\ell).

(2.18)

By setting

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{\epsilon}(h)=\Big(\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{3}{2}}h^{\prime}\Big)^{\prime}\ -\ \Bigg\{\Big(\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(t,\theta)\big]^{\frac{3}{2}}\Big)_{tt}\Big|_{t=0}\,-\,2\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{3}{2}}\kappa^{2}\Bigg\}h,

(2.19)

(see (
8.7
) for the expression of
\mathbb{F}_{\epsilon}
in other coordinates),
we say that a stationary curve
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
is
non-degenerate
if
the following problem

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{\epsilon}(h)=0,\quad h(0)=h(\ell),\quad h^{\prime}(0)=h^{\prime}(\ell),

(2.20)

only has the trivial solution
h\equiv 0
.
Inspired by this, to obtain a precise
a priori
estimate for the corresponding in-homogeneous problem in the reduction procedure, we propose the following assumptions

(B1).

For any small
\epsilon
,
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
is a stationary curve.
Moreover, we assume that

\displaystyle{\mathrm{d}}_{\epsilon}\geq\hat{C}_{2}\epsilon^{\alpha}\quad\text{with}\ {\mathrm{d}}_{\epsilon}=\min_{j=1,2,\cdots}|\Lambda_{\epsilon,j}|,

(2.21)

where
\alpha\in(0,1)
is a constant with constraint in (
2.25
), and
\{\Lambda_{\epsilon,j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
are the real eigenvalues of the eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle\mathbb{F}_{\epsilon}(h)=\beta\Lambda_{\epsilon,j}h,\qquad h(0)=h(\ell),\qquad h^{\prime}(0)=h^{\prime}(\ell),

(2.22)

where
\beta
has been given in (
2.9
).

On the other hand, we will also encounter delicate resonance phenomena in the construction of solutions, which were analyzed in
[
21
]
.
For the similar purpose, the following gap condition for
\epsilon
small will be useful.

(B2).

For given
c>0
small, there holds

\displaystyle\Big|j^{2}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}-\lambda_{*}\Big|\geq\frac{c\epsilon}{{\tilde{\ell}}},\quad\forall\,j\in\mathbb{N},

(2.23)

where by recalling the unique positive eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
of the eigenvalue problem (
3.31
), we have used the conventions

\displaystyle\lambda_{*}=\frac{\lambda_{0}}{4\pi^{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\tilde{\ell}}=\int_{0}^{\ell}\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}\theta.

(2.24)

Furthermore, in order to guarantee the successful execution of the reduction process, we require the following condition (The readers can refer to Remark
2.7
for explanations).

(B3).

For
\varrho,\alpha\in(0,1)
given in (
2.5
) and (
2.21
) respectively, they will satisfy

\displaystyle\varrho>\frac{1}{3}+\frac{4}{3}\alpha.

(2.25)

Remark 2.4
.

Note that
{\tilde{\ell}}
has a uniformly positive lower bound due to (
2.15
), i.e.

\displaystyle{\tilde{\ell}}\geq C_{0}>0

(2.26)

where
C_{0}
is a universal constant independent of
\epsilon
.
On the other hand, the assumption in (
2.5
) implies that

\displaystyle{\tilde{\ell}}\leq C\epsilon^{\varrho-1},

(2.27)

where
0<\varrho<1
has been given in (
2.5
).
By combining (
2.27
) and (
2.23
), we obtain

\displaystyle\Big|j^{2}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}-\lambda_{*}\Big|\geq C\epsilon^{2-\varrho},\quad\forall\,j\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.28)

We shall mention that the validity of (
2.23
) will be the case whenever
{\epsilon}/{{\tilde{\ell}}}
is small and away from the critical numbers
{\sqrt{\lambda_{*}}}/j
with
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
, i.e.,
for arbitrarily small fixed
2c<\sqrt{\lambda_{*}}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\notin\Bigg[\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{*}}}{j}-\frac{c}{j^{2}},\ \frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{*}}}{j}+\frac{c}{j^{2}}\Bigg],\quad\forall\,j\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(2.29)

From the mass constraint (
1.9
), there is a relationship between

\epsilon
and
a

given by (
9.8
), i.e.

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon}{\tilde{\ell}}=\frac{\varrho_{0}}{a}\left(1+O\left(\epsilon^{2-\alpha}\right)\right),

(2.30)

where
\alpha\in(0,1)
is given in (
2.21
) and
\varrho_{0}
is the constant defined in (
3.36
).
Under the relation (
2.30
), we make the gap condition for
a

\displaystyle\frac{\varrho_{0}}{a}\notin\Bigg[\frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{*}}}{j}-\frac{2c}{j^{2}},\ \frac{\sqrt{\lambda_{*}}}{j}+\frac{2c}{j^{2}}\Bigg],\quad\forall\,j\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.31)

where
c
is given by (
2.29
).
In fact, (
2.31
) is a sufficient condition for the validity of (
2.23
), see the analysis in Section
9
.
∎

The following is the theorem for the existence of solutions with concentration.

Theorem 2.5
.

Assume that
N=2
, the smooth simple closed curves
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
and the potential
W
satisfy the assumptions in

(A1)
-
(A2)
and
(B1)
-
(B3)
.
For problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) with relation between
a
and
\epsilon
in (
2.30
), there exists a positive solution
u_{\epsilon}
with the following properties.

\clubsuit

For
{\bf y}
given by (
2.4
) in the neighbourhood of
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
,

u_{\epsilon}
has the local profile

u_{\epsilon}({\bf y})\,=\,\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\,U\Bigg(\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\frac{t}{\epsilon}\Bigg)\big(1+o(1)\big),

(2.32)

where
U
is the function given in (
2.8
).

\clubsuit

The solution
u_{\epsilon}
satisfies globally

u_{\epsilon}({\bf y})\,\leq\,C\exp\Big(-\frac{c_{0}}{\epsilon}{\mathrm{dist}}({\bf y},\Gamma_{\epsilon})\Big),

where
c_{0}
and
C
are two positive constants independent of
\epsilon
.
∎

For the convenience of readers, we go back to problem (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) and provide another version of Theorem
2.5
.
By slight abuse of notation, we use
\Gamma^{\lambda}
to denote the curve
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
by the relation (
1.7
).

Theorem 2.6
.

Assume that
N=2
, the smooth simple closed curves
\Gamma^{\lambda}
and the potential function
W
satisfy
(A1)
-
(A2)
and
(B1)
-
(B3)
.
There exists a large constant
a^{*}>0
such that for all
a>a^{*}
satisfying gap condition (
2.31
),
the parameter
\lambda
in (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) can be chosen by the relations (
1.7
) and (
2.30
).
Moreover, problem (
1.3
)-(
1.4
) has a positive solution
\tilde{u}_{\lambda}
with the following properties

\clubsuit

For
{\bf y}
given by (
2.4
) in the neighbourhood of
\Gamma^{\lambda}
,

\tilde{u}_{\lambda}
takes the form

\tilde{u}_{\lambda}({\bf y})\,=\,\sqrt{\frac{-\lambda}{a}}\,\Bigg[1-\frac{1}{\lambda}W(0,\theta)\Bigg]^{1/2}\,U\Bigg(\Bigg[1-\frac{1}{\lambda}W(0,\theta)\Bigg]^{1/2}\,\frac{t}{\sqrt{-1/\lambda}}\Bigg)\big(1+o(1)\big),

(2.33)

where
U
is the function given in (
2.8
).

\clubsuit

For a number
c_{0}>0,\,\tilde{u}_{\lambda}
satisfies globally

\tilde{u}_{\lambda}({\bf y})\,\leq\,C\sqrt{\frac{-\lambda}{a}}\,\exp\Big(-\frac{c_{0}}{\sqrt{-\lambda}}{\mathrm{dist}}({\bf y},\Gamma_{\lambda})\Big),

where
c_{0}
and
C
are two positive constants independent of
\lambda
.
∎

2.3.
Outline of the strategy and further comments

The substitution

u({\bf y})\,=\,v(y)\quad\mbox{with}\quad y={\bf y}/\epsilon,

(2.34)

in problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) will imply that

\displaystyle-\Delta v+\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(\epsilon y)\big]v-|v|^{2}v\,=\,0,\quad\forall\,y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2},

(2.35)

and

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}|v|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}y\,=\,a.

(2.36)

\clubsuit
In Sections
3.1
-
3.2
, we will introduce the Fermi coordinates and write down equation (
2.35
) in local forms,
see (
3.1
) as well as (
3.29
).
After that, some preliminary facts on the profile function
U
, and some conventions will be given in Section
3.3
.

\clubsuit
Section
4
is devoted to the proof of Theorem
2.2
,
which is constituted by a Pohozaev type identity in Lemma
4.1
and delicate analysis to estimate all terms of the identity.

\clubsuit
On the other hand, the basic philosophy of the construction procedure of a solution to problem (
2.35
)-(
2.36
) and the proof of
Theorem
2.5
will be organized in the following way: we first construct a solution with concentration to equation (
2.35
) by refining the approach presented in
[
21
]
,
and then choose suitable
\epsilon
to fulfill the constraint (
2.36
).
More words for the explanations of details will be provided in the following.

(1).

In Section
5
, we first make a try to construct a local approximation solution to (
2.35
).
In order to improve the approximation, suitable correction terms will be added and a good approximate solution
w_{2}
will be given in (
5.40
),
which is involving two free parameters
f
and
e
.

(2).

The inner-outer gluing scheme from
[
21
]
will be set up in Section
6
.
By means of this useful method, we can deduce the projected version of (
2.35
), see problem (
6.13
)-(
6.15
), and then give the existence together with Lipschitz-continuity of its solutions in Proposition
6.6
.

(3).

In order to get a real solution to (
2.35
), the well-known infinite-dimensional reduction method will be needed in Section
7
to derive a nonlinear coupled system of second order differential equations of
f
and
e
, see (
7.3
)-(
7.3
).
The key step is to find the parameter pair
(f,e)
by solving that system, which will be done in Section
8
.

(4).

In order to fulfill the mass constraint (
2.36
), in Section
9
we shall substitute the solution constructed in the above steps into it and then find the relation between the parameters
a
and
\epsilon
, in such a way that
\epsilon
can be settle down for fixed
a
.

We shall mention that we may use the assumptions in
(A1)
-
(A2)
and
(B1)
-
(B3)

without any further notification in Sections
5
through Section
9
as well as Appendix
A
.

To conclude this section, here are more words to explain Theorem
2.5
and Theorem
2.6
and discuss the difficulties encountered in the procedure of constructing solutions with higher dimensional concentration.

Remark 2.7
.

In local coordinates
(s,z)
given in (
3.4
), problem (
2.35
) can be expressed as in (
3.1
), and its linearization at the profile
U(s)
is

\displaystyle\phi_{ss}+\phi_{zz}-\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\big]\phi+3U^{2}(s)\phi+\big(\epsilon\kappa-\epsilon^{2}\kappa^{2}s\big)\phi_{s}+B_{0}(\phi)=0,

(2.37)

where
s\in(-M_{0}/\epsilon,\,M_{0}/\epsilon),\ z\in\big[0,\ell/\epsilon\big)
.
As done in
[
21
]
, if we neglect the role of
\epsilon^{2}W
and the terms of higher orders in
\epsilon
, the linearization of problem (
2.35
) at the profile
U(s)
has the local form

\phi_{zz}+\phi_{ss}-\phi+3U^{2}(s)\phi=0,\quad s\in{\mathbb{R}},\ z\in\big[0,\ell/\epsilon\big).

There exist two types of eigenvalues

\displaystyle\Big(\frac{2j\pi\epsilon}{\ell}\Big)^{2},\qquad\lambda_{0}\,-\,\Big(\frac{2j\pi\epsilon}{\ell}\Big)^{2},\qquad j\in{\mathbb{N}}^{+},

(2.38)

correspond respectively to the eigenfunctions

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{1,j}=U^{\prime}(s)\Bigg[\mathbf{c}_{1j}\sin\Big(\frac{2j\pi\epsilon z}{\ell}\Big)\,+\,\mathbf{c}_{2j}\cos\Big(\frac{2j\pi\epsilon z}{\ell}\Big)\Bigg],

(2.39)

\displaystyle\Upsilon_{2,j}={\mathcal{Z}}(s)\Bigg[\mathbf{c}_{3j}\sin\Big(\frac{2j\pi\epsilon z}{\ell}\Big)\,+\,\mathbf{c}_{4j}\cos\Big(\frac{2j\pi\epsilon z}{\ell}\Big)\Bigg],

(2.40)

where
\lambda_{0}
is the unique positive eigenvalue with corresponding eigenfunction
{\mathcal{Z}}
to the eigenvalue problem (
3.31
),
and
\mathbf{c}_{1j},\,\mathbf{c}_{2j},\,\mathbf{c}_{3j},\,\mathbf{c}_{4j}
are constants independent of
\epsilon
.
The effects of
\Upsilon_{1,j}
and
\Upsilon_{2,j}
will lead to the resonance phenomena.
In
[
21
]
, in order to deal with the resonance, they introduced two free parameters
f
and
e
in the Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction procedure.
Of course, the reduced system of nonlinear differential equations involving the parameters
f
and
e
will inherit the resonance (The readers can refer to (
7.3
)-(
7.3
), as well as (
8.1
)-(
8.2
) and (
8.12
)),
which is solvable under non-degeneracy condition and the gap condition similar as (
2.20
) and (
2.28
).

However, the length
\ell
of the curve may depend on
\epsilon
and the term
\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)
appears in the operator
{\mathbb{F}}
in (
2.19
).
These will lead to more complicated situation: the eigenvalues
\Lambda_{\epsilon,j}
of problem (
2.22
) depend on
\epsilon
and may approach zero
(As a result, the linear problem (
8.1
)-(
8.2
) may have resonance).
In order to get a good a priori estimate for
f
as in (
8.3
) so that the reduction procedure
can be carried out in Section
8
, we impose further the assumption (
2.21
) in
(B1)
to handle the resonance caused by the effect
\Upsilon_{1,j}
.

Moreover, we recall that the gap condition in (
2.23
) relies heavily on the weighted length
{\tilde{\ell}}
, which is also depends on
\epsilon
.
Whence, the gap condition (
2.23
) in
(B2)
plus the constraint (
2.5
) in
(A2)
will be used to deal with the resonance given by
\Upsilon_{2,j}
.
The readers can refer to Proposition
8.2
.

Therefore, we will encounter more delicate resonance phenomena than that in
[
21
]
,
and we also impose the constraint
(B3)
to balance the competition between the above two resonance phenomena
in such a way that we can save the procedure of construction of approximate solution, which is enough for the resolution theory involving gluing procedure and the reduction method.

Based on the above reasons, we will introduce new norms
\|\cdot\|_{*}
and
\|\cdot\|_{**}
in (
3.43
)-(
3.44
) to do estimates for
f
and
e
.
Besides, in Section
6
, we also define a weighted Sobolev norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{2}_{*}(\mathfrak{S})}
in (
6.20
) to obtain good estimates for the perturbation function.
We shall mention that the adoption of these new norms significantly contributes to the success of the reduction process.
∎

Remark 2.8
.

Since the length
\ell
of curve
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
is not uniformly bounded, it is difficult for us to make a prior estimates to functions involving
\theta
with
\theta\in[0,\ell)
. To overcome this difficulty, we perform the following Liouville transformation

\displaystyle\tau\,=\,\frac{2\pi}{\tilde{\ell}}\int_{0}^{\theta}\beta(\xi)\,{\mathrm{d}}\xi\qquad\mbox{with}\quad{\tilde{\ell}}\,=\,\int_{0}^{\ell}\beta(\xi)\,{\mathrm{d}}\xi,

and consequently get the bijective map

\displaystyle\theta\in[0,\ell)\mapsto\tau\in[0,2\pi),

see (
3.20
).
We obtain the expression of the equation (
2.35
) in the new coordinates
(x,\tau)
with
x
in (
3.24
), and further derive the expression of the error function
S(w_{2})
, seeing (
5.3
), where
w_{2}
is given in (
5.40
).
By projecting the error function onto
\mathcal{Z}
and
U
respectively, we obtain the reduced equations (
7.3
)-(
7.3
) on the coordinate
\tau
. To ensure the solvability of reduced equations and successfully implement the reduction process, we need the conditions
(B1)
-
(B3)
.
For a detailed analysis of how conditions
(B1)
-
(B3)
affect the solvability of the reduced equations, please refer to Remark
2.7
.
∎

Remark 2.9
.

In Theorem
2.5
, we construct non-radial high-dimensional concentrated solutions to problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
), which pioneers the application of infinite-dimensional Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction method to investigate nonlinear elliptic problems with mass constraint.
Moreover, we clarify the effect of the length of the concentrating curve in the higher dimensional concentration phenomenon of problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
), and construct solutions concentrating at
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
. The results provided in Theorem
2.5
and Theorem
2.6
differ significantly from those in
[
21
]
and its references
[
42
,
47
,
48
,
49
,
50
,
51
]
. Furthermore, the results provided in this paper significantly complement the application scope of the infinite-dimensional Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction theory introduced in
[
21
]
.
∎

3.
Some preliminaries and preparing works

3.1.
The local forms of the equations in Fermi coordinates

In the Fermi coordinates
(t,\theta)
given in (
2.4
), the Riemannian metric of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
can be locally written as

\mathfrak{g}\,=\,{\mathrm{d}}t\otimes{\mathrm{d}}t\ +\ \big(1+\kappa(\theta)t\big)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\theta\otimes{\mathrm{d}}\theta.

The Laplace-Beltrami operator has the form

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle\,=\,\frac{1}{1+\kappa(\theta)t}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Bigg[\big(1+\kappa(\theta)t\big)\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Bigg]\ +\ \frac{1}{1+\kappa(\theta)t}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{1+\kappa(\theta)t}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\,=\,\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial t^{2}}+\frac{\kappa(\theta)}{1+\kappa(\theta)t}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\ +\ \frac{1}{\big(1+\kappa(\theta)t\big)^{2}}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial\theta^{2}}\ -\ \frac{t\kappa^{\prime}(\theta)}{\big(1+\kappa(\theta)t\big)^{3}}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}.

(3.1)

It is trivial that equation (
1.8
) takes the local form

\displaystyle\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{1+\kappa(\theta)t}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Bigg[\big(1+\kappa(\theta)t\big)\frac{\partial}{\partial t}u\Bigg]\ +\ \frac{\epsilon^{2}}{1+\kappa(\theta)t}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{1+\kappa(\theta)t}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}u\Bigg]

\displaystyle\ -\ \big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(t,\theta)\big]u\ +\ |u|^{2}u=0.

(3.2)

In the sequel, we will denote

\tilde{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}\,=\,\epsilon^{-1}\Gamma_{\epsilon},

(3.3)

and set the natural stretched coordinates in the neighbourhood of the curve
\tilde{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
,

(s,z)\,=\,\epsilon^{-1}\big(t,\,\theta\big),

(3.4)

which are defined for

s\in\Big(-\frac{M_{0}}{\epsilon},\frac{M_{0}}{\epsilon}\Big),\ \ \ z\in\Big[0,\frac{\ell}{\epsilon}\Big).

(3.5)

Hence, equation (
2.35
) has the local form

\displaystyle\frac{1}{1+\epsilon\kappa s}\frac{\partial}{\partial s}\Big[\big(1+\epsilon\kappa s\big)\frac{\partial}{\partial s}v\Big]+\frac{1}{1+\epsilon\kappa s}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}\Big[\frac{1}{1+\epsilon\kappa s}\frac{\partial}{\partial z}v\Big]-\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\big]v+|v|^{2}v=0,

(3.6)

which can also be written in the following way

\displaystyle v_{ss}+v_{zz}+B_{1}(v)-\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\big]v+|v|^{2}v\,=\,0,

(3.7)

where

\displaystyle B_{1}(v)

\displaystyle\,=\,\frac{\epsilon\kappa(\epsilon z)}{1+\epsilon\kappa(\epsilon z)s}v_{s}+\Big[\frac{1}{(1+\epsilon\kappa(\epsilon z)s)^{2}}-1\Big]v_{zz}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}s\kappa^{\prime}(\epsilon z)}{(1+\epsilon\kappa(\epsilon z)s)^{3}}v_{z}.

(3.8)

In the neighborhood of
\tilde{\Gamma}_{\epsilon}
, by taking the Taylor expansion,
we have

\displaystyle 1+\epsilon^{2}W(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\,=\,

\displaystyle 1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\epsilon z)+\epsilon^{3}sW_{t}(0,\epsilon z)+\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{4}s^{2}W_{tt}(0,\epsilon z)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{6}\epsilon^{5}s^{3}W_{ttt}(0,\epsilon z)+\frac{1}{24}\epsilon^{6}s^{4}W_{tttt}\big(\hbar(\epsilon z)\epsilon s,\,\epsilon z\big),

(3.9)

where
\hbar(\theta)
is a smooth function with
|\hbar(\theta)|\leq 1,\,\forall\,\theta\in[0,\ell)
.
On the other hand, it is trivial that

\displaystyle B_{1}(v)

\displaystyle\,=\,\big(\epsilon\kappa-\epsilon^{2}\kappa^{2}s\big)v_{s}+B_{0}(v),

where

\displaystyle B_{0}(v)\,=\,\epsilon^{3}s^{2}\kappa^{3}(\epsilon z)a_{3}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)v_{s}\,+\,\epsilon s\kappa a_{2}v_{zz}\,+\,\epsilon^{2}s\kappa^{\prime}(\epsilon z)a_{1}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)v_{z},

(3.10)

with

\displaystyle a_{3}(t,\theta)=\frac{1}{1+t\kappa(\theta)},\qquad a_{2}(t,\theta)=\frac{-2-t\kappa(\theta)}{(1+t\kappa(\theta))^{2}},\qquad a_{1}(t,\theta)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{(1+t\kappa(\theta))^{3}}.

(3.11)

The assumptions in (
2.3
) imply that

a_{1},a_{2}
and
a_{3}
are three smooth bounded functions on the following domain

\Big\{\,(t,\theta)\,:\,|t|<M_{0},\quad\theta\in[0,\ell)\,\Big\}

provided that the constant
M_{0}
in (
2.4
) is chosen sufficiently small.

By using the above calculations, we derive from (
3.7
) to get

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}(v):=\,

\displaystyle v_{ss}+v_{zz}-\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\epsilon z)\big]v+v^{3}+\big(\epsilon\kappa-\epsilon^{2}\kappa^{2}s\big)v_{s}+B_{0}(v)-\epsilon^{3}sW_{t}(0,\epsilon z)v

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{4}s^{2}W_{tt}(0,\epsilon z)v-\frac{1}{6}\epsilon^{5}s^{3}W_{ttt}(0,\epsilon z)v-\frac{1}{24}\epsilon^{6}s^{4}W_{tttt}\big(\hbar(\epsilon z)\epsilon s,\,\epsilon z\big)v\,=\,0,

(3.12)

where
\epsilon s\in(-M_{0},M_{0}),\,\epsilon z\in[0,\ell)
.

3.2.
Setting up near the curves

To prove Theorem
2.5
, we need to reformulate problem (
2.35
) in suitable coordinates. In this section, we will do this work step by step.

Step 1.

By recalling the function
\beta
in (
2.9
),
we define a function
{\tilde{v}}({\tilde{s}},z)
by the relation

\displaystyle v(s,z)\,=\,\beta(\epsilon z){\tilde{v}}({\tilde{s}},z)\qquad\text{with}\quad{\tilde{s}}\,=\,\beta(\epsilon z)s.

(3.13)

We will express equation (
3.1
) in terms of new coordinates
({\tilde{s}},z)
by the following preparations.
It’s easy to get that

\displaystyle\partial_{z}{\tilde{s}}\,=\,\epsilon\beta^{\prime}s\,=\,\epsilon\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}{\tilde{s}},\qquad\partial_{zz}{\tilde{s}}\,=\,\epsilon^{2}\beta^{\prime\prime}s\,=\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta}{\tilde{s}}.

After that, there hold

\displaystyle v_{s}

\displaystyle\,=\,\beta^{2}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}},\qquad\qquad v_{ss}\,=\,\beta^{3}{\tilde{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}},\qquad\qquad v_{z}\,=\,\epsilon\beta^{\prime}{\tilde{v}}+\epsilon\beta^{\prime}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\beta{\tilde{v}}_{z},\qquad\qquad

\displaystyle v_{zz}

\displaystyle\,=\,\epsilon^{2}\beta^{\prime\prime}{\tilde{v}}+2\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+2\epsilon\beta^{\prime}{\tilde{v}}_{z}+2\epsilon\beta^{\prime}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{z{\tilde{s}}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\tilde{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}+\epsilon^{2}\beta^{\prime\prime}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\beta{\tilde{v}}_{zz}.

Furthermore, for later use, we also compute

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime}(\theta)\,=\,\frac{\epsilon^{2}W_{\theta}(0,\theta)}{2\beta(\theta)}\,=\,\frac{\epsilon^{2}W_{\theta}(0,\theta)}{2\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}},

(3.14)

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}(\theta)\,=\,\frac{\,\epsilon^{2}W_{\theta\theta}(0,\theta)\,}{2\beta(\theta)}\,-\,\frac{\,\epsilon^{4}|W_{\theta}(0,\theta)|^{2}\,}{4\beta^{3}(\theta)}\,=\,\frac{\,\epsilon^{2}W_{\theta\theta}(0,\theta)\,}{2\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,-\,\frac{\,\epsilon^{4}|W_{\theta}(0,\theta)|^{2}\,}{4\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{3}{2}}},

(3.15)

and

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}(\theta)\,=\,\frac{\,\epsilon^{2}W_{\theta\theta\theta}(0,\theta)\,}{2\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,-\,\frac{3\epsilon^{4}W_{\theta\theta}(0,\theta)\,W_{\theta}(0,\theta)}{4\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{3}{2}}}\,+\,\frac{\,3\epsilon^{6}|W_{\theta}(0,\theta)|^{3}\,}{8\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(0,\theta)\big]^{\frac{5}{2}}}.

(3.16)

The combination of the above formulas and the assumptions in (
2.1.2
) will imply that

\displaystyle|\beta^{\prime}|\,\leq\,C\beta,\qquad|\beta^{\prime\prime}|\,\leq\,C\beta,\qquad|\beta^{\prime\prime\prime}|\,\leq\,C\beta.

(3.17)

Finally, dividing
{\mathcal{D}}(v)
by
\beta^{3}
, we conclude that problem (
3.1
) would be transformed to

\displaystyle{\tilde{S}}({\tilde{v}}):\,=\,{\tilde{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}-{\tilde{v}}+{\tilde{v}}^{3}+\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}{\tilde{v}}_{zz}+B_{3}({\tilde{v}})\,=\,0,

(3.18)

with

\displaystyle B_{3}({\tilde{v}})\,=\,

\displaystyle\Bigg[\epsilon\frac{\kappa}{\,\beta\,}\,-\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\beta^{2}}{\tilde{s}}\Bigg]{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{v}}+\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{2|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\epsilon\frac{2\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{v}}_{z}+\epsilon\frac{2\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{z{\tilde{s}}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\tilde{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}-\epsilon^{3}\frac{W_{t}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{4}\frac{W_{tt}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\tilde{v}}+B_{2}({\tilde{v}}),

where
\epsilon{\tilde{s}}\in(-M_{0},M_{0}),\,\epsilon z\in[0,\ell)
.
In the above formula, we have denoted

\displaystyle B_{2}({\tilde{v}})\,=\,

\displaystyle-\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{6}\frac{\,\,W_{ttt}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{5}}{\tilde{s}}^{3}{\tilde{v}}-\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{24}\frac{\,\,W_{tttt}\big(\hbar(\epsilon z)\epsilon s,\,\epsilon z\big)}{\beta^{6}}{\tilde{s}}^{4}{\tilde{v}}+\frac{1}{\,\beta^{3}\,}B_{0}(v)

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle-\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{6}\frac{\,\,W_{ttt}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{5}}{\tilde{s}}^{3}{\tilde{v}}-\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{24}\frac{\,\,W_{tttt}\big(\hbar(\epsilon z)\epsilon s,\,\epsilon z\big)}{\beta^{6}}{\tilde{s}}^{4}{\tilde{v}}+\epsilon^{3}\kappa^{3}(\epsilon z)a_{3}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\frac{1}{\,\beta^{3}\,}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}

\displaystyle\,+\,\epsilon\kappa(\epsilon z)a_{2}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\Bigg[\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}+\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{2|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{5}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\epsilon\frac{2\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{z}+\epsilon\frac{2\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\tilde{v}}_{z{\tilde{s}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\epsilon^{2}\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{5}}{\tilde{s}}^{3}{\tilde{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\frac{1}{\,\beta^{3}\,}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{zz}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\,+\,\epsilon^{2}a_{1}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\kappa^{\prime}(\epsilon z)\Bigg[\epsilon\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}+\epsilon\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\frac{1}{\,\beta^{3}\,}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{z}\Bigg],

(3.19)

where the linear operator
B_{0}(v)
and the function
\hbar
are defined in (
3.10
), (
3.1
) respectively.
In the above, the terms
\beta^{\prime}
and
\beta^{\prime\prime}
have been calculated in (
3.14
) and (
3.15
).

Step 2.

By recalling the definition of
{\tilde{\ell}}
in (
2.24
), we take the transformation

\displaystyle\tau(z)\,=\,\frac{2\pi}{\tilde{\ell}}\int_{0}^{\epsilon z}\beta(\xi)\,{\mathrm{d}}\xi,

(3.20)

and

\displaystyle{\tilde{v}}({\tilde{s}},z)\,=\,{\hat{v}}({\tilde{s}},\tau).

(3.21)

The above setting will imply that

\displaystyle\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}{{\mathrm{d}}z}\,=\,\epsilon\frac{2\pi}{\tilde{\ell}}\beta(\epsilon z),\qquad\frac{{\mathrm{d}}^{2}\tau}{{\mathrm{d}}z^{2}}\,=\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\beta^{\prime}(\epsilon z),

\displaystyle{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}({\tilde{s}},z)\,=\,{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}({\tilde{s}},\tau),\qquad{\tilde{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}({\tilde{s}},z)\,=\,{\hat{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}({\tilde{s}},\tau),

\displaystyle{\tilde{v}}_{z}({\tilde{s}},z)\,=\,\epsilon\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\beta(\epsilon z){\hat{v}}_{\tau}({\tilde{s}},\tau),\qquad{\tilde{v}}_{z{\tilde{s}}}({\tilde{s}},z)\,=\,\epsilon\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\beta(\epsilon z){\hat{v}}_{\tau{\tilde{s}}}({\tilde{s}},\tau),

and

\displaystyle{\tilde{v}}_{zz}({\tilde{s}},z)\,=\,

\displaystyle\epsilon\partial_{z}\Big[\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\beta(\epsilon z){\hat{v}}_{\tau}({\tilde{s}},\tau)\Big]\,=\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\beta^{\prime}{\hat{v}}_{\tau}({\tilde{s}},\tau)\,+\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{4\pi^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}^{2}}}\beta^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tau\tau}({\tilde{s}},\tau).

We substitute the above results into (
3.18
) and then obtain

\displaystyle 0={\tilde{S}}({\tilde{v}})\,=\,

\displaystyle{\tilde{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}-{\tilde{v}}+{\tilde{v}}^{3}+\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}{\tilde{v}}_{zz}+\Bigg[\epsilon\frac{\kappa}{\,\beta\,}\,-\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\beta^{2}}{\tilde{s}}\Bigg]{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{v}}+\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{2|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\epsilon\frac{2\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{v}}_{z}

\displaystyle+\epsilon\frac{2\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{z{\tilde{s}}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\tilde{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}_{\tilde{s}}-\epsilon^{3}\frac{W_{t}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{v}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{4}\frac{W_{tt}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\tilde{v}}+B_{2}({\tilde{v}})

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle{\hat{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}-{\hat{v}}+{\hat{v}}^{3}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\Bigg[\frac{4\pi^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}\beta^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tau\tau}+\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\beta^{\prime}{\hat{v}}_{\tau}\Bigg]+\Big[\epsilon\frac{\kappa}{\,\beta\,}\,-\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\beta^{2}}{\tilde{s}}\Big]{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\hat{v}}+\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{2|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{2}}\frac{4\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}{\hat{v}}_{\tau}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{2}}\frac{4\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}_{\tau{\tilde{s}}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}-\epsilon\frac{\epsilon^{2}W_{t}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{\epsilon^{2}W_{tt}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}+B_{2}({\tilde{v}})

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle{\hat{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}-{\hat{v}}+{\hat{v}}^{3}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{4\pi^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}{\hat{v}}_{\tau\tau}\,+\,\epsilon\frac{\kappa}{\beta}\Bigg[{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\frac{2}{3}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}\Bigg]+\epsilon^{2}\frac{6\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}\frac{1}{\beta}{\hat{v}}_{\tau}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{4\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{2}}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}_{\tau{\tilde{s}}}

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\Big[\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}\,+\,\frac{2|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}\,-\,\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\Big]{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\hat{v}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}-\frac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{\epsilon^{2}W_{tt}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}+B_{2}({\tilde{v}}),

(3.22)

where
\epsilon{\tilde{s}}\in(-M_{0},M_{0}),\,\tau\in[0,2\pi)
.
In (
3.22
), we have used the stationary condition in (
2.18
).
In the coordinates
(\tilde{s},\tau)
, the expression of
B_{2}({\tilde{v}})
in (
3.22
) has the form

\displaystyle B_{2}({\tilde{v}})\,=\,

\displaystyle-\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{6}\frac{\,\,W_{ttt}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{5}}{\tilde{s}}^{3}{\hat{v}}-\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{24}\frac{\,\,W_{tttt}\big(\hbar(\epsilon z)\epsilon s,\,\epsilon z\big)}{\beta^{6}}{\tilde{s}}^{4}{\hat{v}}+\epsilon^{3}\kappa^{3}(\epsilon z)a_{3}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\frac{1}{\,\beta^{3}\,}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}

\displaystyle\,+\,\epsilon\kappa(\epsilon z)a_{2}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\Bigg\{\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}+\epsilon^{2}\,\frac{2|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{5}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{2\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\beta{\hat{v}}_{\tau}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{2\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\beta{\hat{v}}_{\tau{\tilde{s}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\epsilon^{2}\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{5}}{\tilde{s}}^{3}{\hat{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{1}{\,\beta^{3}\,}{\tilde{s}}\Bigg[\frac{4\pi^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}\beta^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tau\tau}+\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\beta^{\prime}{\hat{v}}_{\tau}\Bigg]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\,+\,\epsilon^{2}a_{1}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\kappa^{\prime}(\epsilon z)\Bigg\{\epsilon\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}+\epsilon\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\epsilon\frac{1}{\,\beta^{3}\,}{\tilde{s}}\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\beta{\hat{v}}_{\tau}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle-\frac{\epsilon^{5}}{6}\frac{\,\,W_{ttt}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{5}}{\tilde{s}}^{3}{\hat{v}}-\frac{\epsilon^{6}}{24}\frac{\,\,W_{tttt}\big(\hbar(\epsilon z)\epsilon s,\,\epsilon z\big)}{\beta^{6}}{\tilde{s}}^{4}{\hat{v}}+\epsilon^{3}\kappa^{3}(\epsilon z)a_{3}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\frac{1}{\,\beta^{3}\,}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}

\displaystyle\,+\,\epsilon^{3}\kappa(\epsilon z)a_{2}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\Bigg\{\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}+\,\frac{2|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{5}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\frac{6\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}_{\tau}+\frac{4\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{3}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tau\tilde{s}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{5}}{\tilde{s}}^{3}{\hat{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}{\tilde{s}}}+\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\frac{4\pi^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\beta}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}_{\tau\tau}\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\,+\,\epsilon^{3}a_{1}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\kappa^{\prime}(\epsilon z)\Bigg\{\epsilon\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}+\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}{\tilde{s}}^{2}{\hat{v}}_{\tilde{s}}+\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}{\tilde{s}}{\hat{v}}_{\tau}\Bigg\}.

(3.23)

Step 3.

We define a function
w(x,\tau)
by the relation

\displaystyle{\hat{v}}({\tilde{s}},\tau)\,=\,w(x,\tau)\qquad\text{with}\quad x\,=\,{\tilde{s}}-f(\tau)=\beta s-f(\tau),

(3.24)

where
f:[0,2\pi)\mapsto{\mathbb{R}}
with the constraint (
3.43
) is a function to be chosen later (see Sections
7
and
8
) and satisfies the boundary conditions

\quad f(0)\,=\,f(2\pi),\quad f^{\prime}(0)\,=\,f^{\prime}(2\pi).

It’s easy to get that

\displaystyle\partial_{\tau}x

\displaystyle\,=\,f^{\prime},\qquad\partial_{\tau\tau}x\,=\,f^{\prime\prime}.

After that, there hold

\displaystyle{\hat{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}}\,=\,w_{x},\qquad\qquad{\hat{v}}_{ss}\,=\,w_{xx},

\displaystyle{\hat{v}}_{\tau}\,=\,-f^{\prime}w_{x}+w_{\tau},\qquad\qquad{\hat{v}}_{{\tilde{s}}\tau}\,=\,-f^{\prime}w_{xx}+w_{x\tau},

\displaystyle{\hat{v}}_{\tau\tau}\,=\,|f^{\prime}|^{2}w_{xx}-f^{\prime\prime}w_{x}+w_{\tau\tau}-2f^{\prime}w_{x\tau}.

Therefore, we write (
3.22
) in the form

\displaystyle\,w_{xx}-w+w^{3}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}4\pi^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}w_{\tau\tau}+\epsilon^{2}\frac{4\pi^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}\Big[|f^{\prime}|^{2}w_{xx}-f^{\prime\prime}w_{x}-2f^{\prime}w_{x\tau}\Big]

\displaystyle+\epsilon\frac{\kappa}{\,\beta\,}\Big[w_{x}+\frac{2}{3}(x+f)w\Big]+\epsilon^{2}\frac{6\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}\frac{1}{\beta}\big[-f^{\prime}w_{x}+w_{\tau}\big]

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\frac{4\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{2}}(x+f)\big[-f^{\prime}w_{xx}+w_{x\tau}\big]+\epsilon^{2}\Big[\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}\,+\,\frac{2|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}\,-\,\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\Big](x+f)w_{x}

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}w+\epsilon^{2}\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}(x+f)^{2}w_{xx}-\frac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{\epsilon^{2}W_{tt}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{4}}(x+f)^{2}w+B_{2}({\tilde{v}})=0,

(3.25)

where
(x,\tau)\in{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,2\pi)
.

For the simplification of notation in (
3.25
), we denote

\displaystyle B_{4}(w)=\epsilon\frac{\kappa}{\,\beta\,}\Big[w_{x}+\frac{2}{3}(x+f)w\Big],

(3.26)

and

\displaystyle B_{5}(w)=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\frac{4\pi^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}\Big[|f^{\prime}|^{2}w_{xx}-f^{\prime\prime}w_{x}-2f^{\prime}w_{x\tau}\Big]+\epsilon^{2}\frac{6\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{2}}\Big[-f^{\prime}w_{x}+w_{\tau}\Big]

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\frac{4\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{2}}(x+f)\big[-f^{\prime}w_{xx}+w_{x\tau}\big]+\epsilon^{2}\Big[\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}\,+\,\frac{2|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}\,-\,\frac{\kappa^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\Big](x+f)w_{x}

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{2}\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{3}}w+\epsilon^{2}\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{4}}(x+f)^{2}w_{xx}-\frac{1}{2}\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{\epsilon^{2}W_{tt}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{4}}(x+f)^{2}w.

(3.27)

For the last term in (
3.25
), by (
3.23
) and the relation
\hat{v}(\tilde{s},\tau)=w(x,\tau)
, we derive

\displaystyle B_{2}({\tilde{v}})\,=\,

\displaystyle-\frac{\epsilon^{3}}{6}\frac{\,\,\epsilon^{2}W_{ttt}(0,\epsilon z)}{\beta^{5}}(x+f)^{3}w-\frac{\epsilon^{4}}{24}\frac{\,\,\epsilon^{2}W_{tttt}\big(\hbar(\epsilon z)\epsilon s,\,\epsilon z\big)}{\beta^{6}}(x+f)^{4}w

\displaystyle+\epsilon^{3}\kappa^{3}(\epsilon z)a_{3}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\frac{1}{\,\beta^{3}\,}(x+f)^{2}w_{x}

\displaystyle\,+\,\epsilon^{3}\kappa(\epsilon z)a_{2}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\Bigg\{\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{4}}(x+f)w+\frac{2|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{5}}(x+f)^{2}w_{x}+\frac{6\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{3}}(x+f)\big[-f^{\prime}w_{x}+w_{\tau}\big]

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{4\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{3}}(x+f)^{2}[-f^{\prime}w_{xx}+w_{x\tau}]+\frac{|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}}{\beta^{5}}(x+f)^{3}w_{xx}+\frac{\beta^{\prime\prime}}{\beta^{4}}(x+f)^{2}w_{x}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+\frac{4\pi^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}\frac{1}{\beta}(x+f)\big[|f^{\prime}|^{2}w_{xx}-f^{\prime\prime}w_{x}+w_{\tau\tau}-2f^{\prime}w_{x\tau}\big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle\,+\,\epsilon^{3}\kappa^{\prime}(\epsilon z)a_{1}(\epsilon s,\epsilon z)\Bigg\{\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}(x+f)w+\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta^{4}}(x+f)^{2}w_{x}+\frac{2\pi}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}(x+f)\big[-f^{\prime}w_{x}+w_{\tau}\big]\Bigg\}

\displaystyle:\,=\,

\displaystyle B_{6}(w).

(3.28)

Note that the coefficients in
B_{4}
,
B_{5}
and
B_{6}
are smooth bounded functions on the following domain

\Big\{\,(s,z)\,:\,|\epsilon s|<M_{0},\quad\epsilon z\in[0,\ell)\,\Big\},

due to the assumptions in (
2.3
) and (
2.6
)-(
2.1.2
).
We finally use (
3.25
) to obtain the equation

\displaystyle S(w):=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\epsilon^{2}4\pi^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}w_{\tau\tau}+w_{xx}-w+w^{3}+B_{4}(w)+B_{5}(w)+B_{6}(w)=0,

(3.29)

where
x\in(-M_{0}/\epsilon,\,M_{0}/\epsilon),\,\tau\in[0,2\pi)
.

3.3.
The block solution
U
and conventions

Recall that
U
is the unique solution to (
2.8
), which is even in
x
, and also enjoys the asymptotic behaviours

\displaystyle\lim_{|x|\rightarrow+\infty}U(x)e^{|x|}=C<+\infty,\qquad\text{and}\qquad\lim_{|x|\rightarrow+\infty}\frac{U(x)}{U^{\prime}(x)}=-1.

(3.30)

Moreover,
U
satisfies the non-degeneracy condition, in the sense that the kernel of the linear operator
-\Delta+1-3U^{2}
in
H^{1}(\mathbb{R})
is spanned by the function
U^{\prime}(x)
.
For the linear eigenvalue problem on
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}})

\displaystyle h{{}^{\prime\prime}}-h+3U^{2}h\,=\,\lambda h\quad\mbox{in }\,\mathbb{R},\,\quad h(\pm\infty)\,=\,0,

(3.31)

it is also well-known that it possesses a unique positive eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
,
with associated eigenfunction
{\mathcal{Z}}
(even and positive) which can be normalized so that

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathcal{Z}}^{2}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x=1.

In fact, a simple computation shows that

\lambda_{0}\,=\,4,\qquad\mathcal{Z}(x)\,=\,\frac{U^{2}(x)}{\sqrt{\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}U^{4}\,{\mathrm{d}}x}\,}.

(3.32)

The reader can refer to
[
21
]
.

It is easy to derive the following relations

\displaystyle 3\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}U^{4}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=4\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}U^{\prime 3}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=12\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}|U^{\prime}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x:=12\varrho_{1},

(3.33)

and

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}xUU^{\prime}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}U^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=-\frac{3}{2}\int_{{\mathbb{R}}}|U^{\prime}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=-\frac{3}{2}\varrho_{1},

(3.34)

where

\displaystyle\varrho_{1}\,:=\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}|U^{\prime}(x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=-\,2\int_{\mathbb{R}}xU^{\prime}(x)U^{\prime\prime}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

(3.35)

For later use, we denote the following constants

\displaystyle\varrho_{0}:=\int_{\mathbb{R}}|U(x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=-\,2\int_{\mathbb{R}}xU(x)U^{\prime}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

(3.36)

\displaystyle\varrho_{2}\,:=\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}|xU^{\prime}(x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x=-\,\frac{2}{3}\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{3}U^{\prime}(x)U^{\prime\prime}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

(3.37)

\displaystyle\varrho_{3}\,:=\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}|xU^{\prime\prime}(x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

(3.38)

\displaystyle\varrho_{4}\,:=\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x^{2}U^{\prime\prime}(x)|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}x,

(3.39)

\displaystyle\varrho_{p}\,:=\,\int_{\mathbb{R}}|U(x)|^{p+1}\,{\mathrm{d}}x.

(3.40)

Moreover, it is trivial that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}x^{2}U(x)U^{\prime\prime}(x)\,{\mathrm{d}}x=\varrho_{0}-\varrho_{2}.

(3.41)

We end this section by providing a collection of notation.
Throughout the paper,
{\mathfrak{S}}
represents the strip in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
of the form

{\mathfrak{S}}=\Big\{(x,\tau):\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}},\quad 0<\tau<2\pi\Big\}.

(3.42)

For the introduction of the parameters
f
and
e
, the reader can refer to (
3.24
)
and (
5.1
).
In all what follows, we will make the following assumptions on the parameters
f
and
e

\displaystyle\|f\|_{*}:\,=\,\left\|\beta f\right\|_{L^{2}(0,2\pi)}+\left\|\frac{\beta}{\tilde{\ell}}f_{\tau}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,2\pi)}+\left\|\frac{\beta}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}f_{\tau\tau}\right\|_{L^{2}(0,2\pi)}\leq\epsilon^{1-\alpha},

(3.43)

\displaystyle\|e\|_{**}:\,=\,\|e\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2\pi)}+\frac{\epsilon}{{\tilde{\ell}}}\|e^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(0,2\pi)}+\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}\|e^{\prime\prime}\|_{L^{2}(0,2\pi)}\leq\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}\varrho+\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha},

(3.44)

where
\beta
has been given in (
2.9
), and the parameters
\varrho
and
\alpha
have been given in (
2.5
) and (
2.21
)
with the requirement in (
2.25
).
By (
2.25
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\|e\|_{**}\leq\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}\varrho+\frac{1}{2}-2\alpha}\ll\epsilon^{1-\alpha},

as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0^{+}
.

Finally, by the norm defined in (
3.43
), we claim that

\displaystyle\left\|\beta f\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2\pi)}\leq C\,{\tilde{\ell}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{*},\qquad\left\|\frac{\beta}{\tilde{\ell}}f_{\tau}\right\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2\pi)}\leq C\,{\tilde{\ell}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{*}.

(3.45)

In fact, we consider the following two cases.

\clubsuit
If

\sup_{\tau\in(0,2\pi)}(\beta f)^{2}\leq 2\inf_{\tau\in(0,2\pi)}(\beta f)^{2},

then

\sup_{\tau\in(0,2\pi)}(\beta f)^{2}\leq 2\inf_{\tau\in(0,2\pi)}(\beta f)^{2}\leq 2\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{0}^{2\pi}(\beta f)^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\leq C\|f\|_{*}^{2}.

Note that there exists a positive constant
{\tilde{\ell}}_{0}
independent of
\epsilon
such that

\displaystyle{\tilde{\ell}}\geq{\tilde{\ell}}_{0},

due to the fact in (
2.15
).
Therefore, there holds

\|(\beta f)^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2\pi)}\leq C{\tilde{\ell}}\|f\|_{*}^{2}.

\clubsuit
We consider the case

\sup_{\tau\in(0,2\pi)}(\beta f)^{2}>2\inf_{\tau\in(0,2\pi)}(\beta f)^{2}.

By denoting
\tau_{0}
the point such that

\big(\beta f\big)^{2}\Big|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}\leq\frac{5}{4}\inf_{\tau\in(0,2\pi)}(\beta f)^{2},

and we obtain

\displaystyle\int^{\tau}_{\tau_{0}}\beta f(\beta f)_{\tau}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\beta f\big)^{2}\Big|^{\tau}_{\tau_{0}}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\beta f\big)^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\big(\beta f\big)^{2}\Big|_{\tau=\tau_{0}},

and then

\displaystyle(\beta f)^{2}\leq\big(\beta f\big)^{2}\Big|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}\,+\,2\int^{\tau}_{\tau_{0}}|\beta f(\beta f)_{\tau}|{\mathrm{d}}\tau.

We get

\displaystyle\sup_{\tau\in(0,2\pi)}(\beta f)^{2}\,\leq\,

\displaystyle\big(\beta f\big)^{2}\Big|_{\tau=\tau_{0}}\,+\,2\int^{2\pi}_{0}|\beta f(\beta f)_{\tau}|{\mathrm{d}}\tau

\displaystyle\,\leq\,

\displaystyle\frac{5}{4}\inf_{\tau\in(0,2\pi)}(\beta f)^{2}\,+\,2\int^{2\pi}_{0}|\beta f(\beta f)_{\tau}|{\mathrm{d}}\tau

\displaystyle\,\leq\,

\displaystyle\frac{5}{8}\sup_{\tau\in(0,2\pi)}(\beta f)^{2}\,+\,2\int^{2\pi}_{0}|\beta f(\beta f)_{\tau}|{\mathrm{d}}\tau.

Hence,

\displaystyle\|(\beta f)^{2}\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2\pi)}\leq C\int^{2\pi}_{0}|\beta f(\beta f)_{\tau}|{\mathrm{d}}\tau.

The term in the right hand side can be estimated as follows

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\beta f||(\beta f)_{\tau}|{\mathrm{d}}\tau

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\tilde{\ell}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\beta f|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\ +\ \frac{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}{\tilde{\ell}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big|\frac{(\beta f)_{\tau}}{\tilde{\ell}}\Big|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\tilde{\ell}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\beta f|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\ +\ \frac{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}{\tilde{\ell}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big|\frac{\beta f_{\tau}}{\tilde{\ell}}\Big|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\ +\ \frac{1}{\tilde{\ell}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big|\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}\Big|^{2}\Big|\frac{{\mathrm{d}}\theta}{{\mathrm{d}}\tau}\Big|^{2}\,|\beta f|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\tilde{\ell}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\beta f|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\ +\ \frac{{\tilde{\ell}}^{2}}{\tilde{\ell}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big|\frac{\beta f_{\tau}}{\tilde{\ell}}\Big|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\ +\ \frac{1}{\tilde{\ell}}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big|\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}\Big|^{2}\Big|\frac{\tilde{\ell}}{2\pi\beta}\Big|^{2}\,|\beta f|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\left(\tilde{\ell}\int_{0}^{2\pi}|\beta f|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\ +\ \tilde{\ell}\int_{0}^{2\pi}\Big|\frac{\beta f_{\tau}}{\tilde{\ell}}\Big|^{2}{\mathrm{d}}\tau\right)

\displaystyle\leq C\tilde{\ell}\|f\|_{*}^{2},

where we have used (
3.17
).
Therefore,

\|\beta f\|_{L^{\infty}(0,2\pi)}\leq C{\tilde{\ell}}^{\frac{1}{2}}\|f\|_{*}.

We can prove the second inequality in (
3.45
) in a similar way.

4.
Proof of Theorem
2.2

As stated in the assumptions of Theorem
2.2
, the solution
u_{\epsilon}
of problem (
1.8
)-(
1.9
) has
a concentration on the curve
\Gamma_{\epsilon}
in the sense of the decomposition in (
2.11
)-(
2.14
).

4.1.
The identities of Pohozaev type

We here provide some identities by mimicking the proof of Pohozaev identity.

Lemma 4.1
.

Suppose that
u=u_{\epsilon}
is a solution of (
1.8
). Then the following identities are valid in the local coordinates given in (
2.4
)

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}\big({1+\kappa t}\big)u_{t}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,+\,\int_{-M}^{M}\big({1+\kappa t}\big)\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(t,\theta)\big]u^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,-\,\int_{-M}^{M}\big({1+\kappa t}\big)u^{4}\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\,=\,\epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}u\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\Big[\frac{1}{1+\kappa t}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}t\ +\ \epsilon^{2}\big(1+\kappa t\big)uu_{t}\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M},

(4.1)

and

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}W_{t}(t,\theta)u^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\ +\ 2\kappa\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(t,\theta)\big]u^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\ -\ \kappa\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)u^{4}\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle=\,-2\epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)u_{t}\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\Big[\frac{1}{1+\kappa t}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,-\,\epsilon^{2}\Big[\big(1+\kappa t\big)u_{t}\Big]^{2}\,\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M}

\displaystyle\quad\,+\,\big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(t,\theta)\big]u^{2}\,\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M}\ -\ \frac{1}{2}\big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}|u|^{4}\,\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M},

(4.2)

where
0<M<M_{0}
is any universal constant.
Consequently,

\displaystyle 2\epsilon^{2}\kappa\int_{-M}^{M}\big({1+\kappa t}\big)u_{t}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,-\,\epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}W_{t}(t,\theta)u^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,-\,\kappa\int_{-M}^{M}\big({1+\kappa t}\big)u^{4}\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\,=\,2\epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big[\big(1+\kappa t\big)u\Big]\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\Big[\frac{1}{1+\kappa t}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}t\ +\ 2\epsilon^{2}\kappa\big(1+\kappa t\big)uu_{t}\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M}\ +\ \epsilon^{2}\Big[\big(1+\kappa t\big)u_{t}\Big]^{2}\,\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M}

\displaystyle\quad\ -\ \big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(t,\theta)\big]u^{2}\,\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M}\ +\ \frac{1}{2}\big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}|u|^{4}\,\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M}.

(4.3)

Proof.

For the proof, we will use the local form of (
1.8
), which has been given in (
3.2
).

Step 1.

Multiplying
(1+\kappa t)\,u
on the both sides of (
3.2
) and integrating, we get

\displaystyle-\ \epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}u\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Bigg[\big(1+\kappa t\big)\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}\Bigg]\,{\mathrm{d}}t\ -\ \epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}u\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\Bigg[\frac{1}{1+\kappa t}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\Bigg]\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle+\ \int_{-M}^{M}\big({1+\kappa t}\big)\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(t,\theta)\big]u^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\ -\ \int_{-M}^{M}\big({1+\kappa t}\big)u^{4}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,=\,0.

(4.4)

By some trivial calculations, we can get

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}-u\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big[\big(1+\kappa t\big)\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}t=\epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}\big({1+\kappa t}\big)u_{t}^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\ -\ \epsilon^{2}\big(1+\kappa t\big)uu_{t}\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M}.

(4.5)

By (
4.1
)-(
4.5
), we have proved (
4.1
).

Step 2.

Multiplying equation (
3.2
) by
(1+\kappa t)^{2}u_{t}
and integrating in the interval
(-M,M)
, we have

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)u_{t}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big[\big(1+\kappa t\big)\frac{\partial u}{\partial t}\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}t\ +\ \epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)u_{t}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\theta}\Big[\frac{1}{1+\kappa t}\frac{\partial u}{\partial\theta}\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\ +\

\displaystyle\int_{-M}^{M}\Big\{-\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(t,\theta)\big]u\,+\,|u|^{2}u\Big\}\big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}u_{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,=\,0.

It is trivial to derive

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)u_{t}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Big[\big(1+\kappa t\big)u_{t}\Big]\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,=\,\epsilon^{2}\frac{1}{2}\Big[\big(1+\kappa t\big)u_{t}\Big]^{2}\,\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M}.

Similarly, we also have

\displaystyle-\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(t,\theta)\big]uu_{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\,=\,\frac{1}{2}\epsilon^{2}\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}W_{t}(t,\theta)u^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t\,+\,\kappa\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(t,\theta)\big]u^{2}{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\quad\,-\,\frac{1}{2}\big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}\big[1+\epsilon^{2}W(t,\theta)\big]u^{2}\,\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M},

and

\displaystyle\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}|u|^{2}uu_{t}\,{\mathrm{d}}t

\displaystyle\,=\,-\frac{1}{2}\kappa\int_{-M}^{M}\big(1+\kappa t\big)|u|^{4}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\ +\ \frac{1}{4}\big(1+\kappa t\big)^{2}|u|^{4}\,\Bigg|_{t=-M}^{M}.

Whence, we obtain the validity of (
4.2
) by combining the above calculations.
Moreover, (
4.3
) is a direct consequence of (
4.1
) and (
4.2
).
∎

4.2.
The estimates of the profile with concentration

For later convenience of notation, we here recall the descriptions of the solutions with concentration,
see (
2.11
)-(
2.14
).

In order to give explicit forms of further estimates, some analysis will be done here.
The expression of
{\mathbf{u}}
in (
2.12
) gives that

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}_{t}=\chi^{\prime}(t)\beta U\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,\epsilon^{-1}\chi(t)\beta\beta U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big),

(4.6)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}_{\theta}=\chi(t)\beta^{\prime}U\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,\epsilon^{-1}\chi(t)\beta\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)t,

(4.7)

and

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}_{tt}=\chi^{\prime\prime}(t)\beta U\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,2\epsilon^{-1}\chi^{\prime}(t)\beta\beta U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,\epsilon^{-2}\chi(t)\beta\beta^{2}U^{\prime\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big),

(4.8)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}_{t\theta}=

\displaystyle\,\chi^{\prime}(t)\beta^{\prime}U\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,\epsilon^{-1}\chi(t)\beta^{\prime}\beta\,U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,\epsilon^{-1}\chi^{\prime}(t)\beta\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)t

\displaystyle\,+\,\epsilon^{-1}\chi(t)\beta\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,\epsilon^{-2}\chi(t)\beta\beta\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)t,

(4.9)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}_{\theta\theta}\,=\,

\displaystyle\chi(t)\beta^{\prime\prime}U\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,2\epsilon^{-1}\chi(t)\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)t\,+\,\epsilon^{-1}\chi(t)\beta\beta^{\prime\prime}U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)t

\displaystyle\,+\,\epsilon^{-2}\chi(t)\beta|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}U^{\prime\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)t^{2},

(4.10)

where the expressions of
\beta^{\prime}
,
\beta^{\prime\prime}
have been given in (
3.14
)-(
3.15
).
Specially, for
|t|\leq M_{1}
, there hold

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}=\beta U\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big),\qquad{\mathbf{u}}_{t}=\epsilon^{-1}\beta\beta U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big),\qquad{\mathbf{u}}_{\theta}=\beta^{\prime}U\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,\epsilon^{-1}\beta\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)t,

(4.11)

and

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}_{tt}=\epsilon^{-2}\beta\beta^{2}U^{\prime\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big),

(4.12)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}_{t\theta}=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-1}\beta^{\prime}\beta\,U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,\epsilon^{-1}\beta\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,\epsilon^{-2}\beta\beta\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)t,

(4.13)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}_{\theta\theta}\,=\,

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}U\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,+\,\frac{2}{\epsilon}\beta^{\prime}\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)t\,+\,\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\beta|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}U^{\prime\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)t^{2}\,+\,\frac{1}{\epsilon}\beta\beta^{\prime\prime}U^{\prime}\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)t.

(4.14)

By setting

{\mathbf{t}}=\beta t/\epsilon,\quad\mbox{ i.e. }t=\epsilon{\mathbf{t}}/\beta,

we have that for
|t|\leq M_{1}
, there hold

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}=\beta U({\mathbf{t}}),\qquad{\mathbf{u}}_{t}=\epsilon^{-1}\beta\beta U^{\prime}({\mathbf{t}}),\qquad{\mathbf{u}}_{\theta}=\beta^{\prime}U({\mathbf{t}})\,+\,\beta^{-1}\beta\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime}({\mathbf{t}}){\mathbf{t}},

(4.15)

and

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}_{tt}=\epsilon^{-2}\beta\beta^{2}U^{\prime\prime}({\mathbf{t}}),\qquad{\mathbf{u}}_{t\theta}=

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-1}\beta^{\prime}\beta\,U^{\prime}({\mathbf{t}})\,+\,\epsilon^{-1}\beta\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime}({\mathbf{t}})\,+\,\epsilon^{-1}\beta\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime\prime}({\mathbf{t}}){\mathbf{t}},

(4.16)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{u}}_{\theta\theta}\,=\,

\displaystyle\beta^{\prime\prime}U({\mathbf{t}})\,+\,2\frac{\beta^{\prime}}{\beta}\beta^{\prime}U^{\prime}({\mathbf{t}}){\mathbf{t}}\,+\,\frac{\beta}{\beta^{2}}|\beta^{\prime}|^{2}U^{\prime\prime}({\mathbf{t}}){\mathbf{t}}^{2}\,+\,\beta^{-1}\beta\beta^{\prime\prime}U^{\prime}({\mathbf{t}}){\mathbf{t}}.

(4.17)

Recall the formulas in (
4.11
)-(
4.14
) and the constants in (
3.36
)-(
3.41
), we first compute

\displaystyle\int_{{\mathbb{R}}^{2}}|{\mathbf{u}}|^{2}\,{\mathrm{d}}y

\displaystyle\,=\,\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{-M_{2}}^{M_{2}}\Big[\,\chi(t)\beta\,U\big(\beta t/\epsilon\big)\,\Big]^{2}(1+\kappa t)\,{\mathrm{d}}t{\mathrm{d}}\theta

\displaystyle\,=\,\int_{0}^{\ell}\int_{-\frac{M_{2}\beta}{\epsilon}}^{\frac{M_{2}\beta}{\epsilon}}\Big[\,\chi\Big(\frac{\epsilon}{\beta}{\mathbf{t}}\Big)\beta\,U({\mathbf{t}})\,\Big]^{2}\big(1+\epsilon\kappa\beta^{-1}{\mathbf{t}}\big)\frac{\epsilon}{\beta}\,{\mathrm{d}}{\mathbf{t}}{\mathrm{d}}\theta
